## Researcher–agent collaboration



Chronological shared conversation. The researcher sees these handoffs, not private tool output. Full tool and model records follow.

### 1. Researcher — initial_request

Please inspect the staged repository and relevant data files, especially capital.csv and any analysis code or notebooks that define the PCA and its 100-year bins. Do not perform the full analysis or give the final hypothesis yet. Report the available files and data structure, propose a reproducible approach to determine symbolic-capital loadings on PC1 and distribution along PC2 across time slices, and flag consequential assumptions or uncertainties (including PCA scaling/sign conventions and whether components mean loadings or scores).

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** initial_inspection; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** The brief asks for a data-grounded hypothesis about symbolic-capital variables in a time-sliced PCA, but no repository inspection or analysis results have been reported yet.

**Open objectives:** Inspect repository and relevant data/code; Propose PCA inspection approach; Identify consequential assumptions and uncertainties

**Agent reply (plan)**

Inspection found only `task.json` and three CSVs under `data/`; no analysis scripts or notebooks are staged. `capital.csv` has 39 rows and 15 columns: BCE, group/color labels, and 12 apparent capital-indicator columns. Its indicator values are mostly strings using decimal commas (for example, `-0,44`), so parsing requires locale-aware decimal conversion. The paired `time_series_data.csv` has 3,601 annual rows, dates CE/calBP, raw sparse indicator series, and interpolated `_inter` series; the capital indicators in it appear populated via interpolation across almost all years. The metadata describes the capital indicators and identifies `group`/`color` as fields dropped after loading, but does not specify PCA implementation, feature scaling, exact bin boundaries, or component orientation.

**Shared evidence:**

Executed repository listing: files are `data/capital.csv`, `data/time_series_data.csv`, `data/pollen_openness_score_Belau_Woserin_Feeser_et_al_2019.csv`, and `task.json`; no code/notebook found. Pandas inspection shows capital shape (39,15) and header `BCE, group, color, ZAxtSchwert, ZDolch, Zhausgr, ZCU_AU, Zamber, ZMonument, ZHort, ZSichel, ZBeil, ZMW, ZKeform, Zkeverz`. First rows are BCE 4300, 4200, 4100, all group EN, with comma-decimal string entries. time_series_data shape (3601,37); raw capital indicators have 3,564 missing values each while interpolated counterparts have none. These are observed structure facts, not PCA results.

**Assumptions:** `capital.csv` is the primary source for the capital PCA because its columns directly represent capital indicators and BCE rows at 100-year intervals.; The time-series table may help explain the annual interpolation but does not itself prove the PCA method.

**Uncertainties:** No PCA code/notebook or explicit bin definition exists among staged files; whether the 39 capital rows are bin values or representatives of groups must be checked against complete data.; PCA may use standardized variables, raw covariance, or already-standardized z values (possibly standardized again); each changes loading magnitudes and potentially rankings.; The question may mean loadings (feature relation to PC1/PC2) or scores (individual slice positions); those interpretations differ. PCA axis signs are arbitrary, so directional descriptions require a documented orientation convention.; It is unclear whether PCA is calculated once for all time slices or independently per 100-year slice, and whether PC2 'distribution' refers to feature loadings or temporal sample scores.

### 2. Researcher — next_task

Thanks; the inventory and uncertainties are clear. Proceed with the main analysis using capital.csv as the primary data, after verifying its values, period/group labels, and 100-year structure. Determine what the question most plausibly means by PC1 values and PC2 distribution, and quantify the symbolic indicators (house size, daggers, hatchets/swords) accordingly. If the intended PCA procedure is not documented, compare defensible candidates (pooled versus per-slice where relevant; standardized versus covariance PCA if consequential), state the chosen interpretation and sign convention explicitly, and avoid treating an arbitrary component sign as intrinsically positive or negative. Report reproducible workflow and evidence for the resulting natural-language hypothesis; do not overstate uncertain procedural details.

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** approach_review; **approach approved:** True

**Basis:** The inspection reports 39 BCE rows with 12 indicator columns in comma-decimal format, no staged PCA code, and unresolved PCA scaling, orientation, and loading-versus-score meanings. The study context identifies the three symbolic indicators and 100-year bins, supporting direct analysis while requiring transparent handling of unspecified method choices.

**Open objectives:** Verify and analyze capital.csv and its time/group organization.; Determine symbolic-indicator behavior on PC1 and distribution on PC2 across time slices with transparent method assumptions.; Submit a data-grounded natural-language hypothesis and reproducible workflow.

**Agent reply (result)**

The most defensible reading is that “values on PC1” means how strongly the three symbolic variables load on the pooled PC1 axis, and “distribution across PC2” means their relation to PC2 plus how the 100-year observations (scores) fall along that axis. In `capital.csv`, all three load positively and moderately on PC1 under a transparent sign orientation: hatchets/swords (ZAxtSchwert) 0.299, daggers (ZDolch) 0.266, and house size (Zhausgr) 0.225 in standardized/correlation PCA. Their PC1 score correlations are about 0.57, 0.51, and 0.43, respectively. PC2 distinguishes house size: it has a moderately strong loading/correlation there (0.327 / 0.500), whereas daggers and hatchets/swords are weakly related (0.074 / 0.113 and 0.054 / 0.083). Thus house size is the symbolic indicator most distributed along PC2; the weapon indicators align mainly with PC1. Interpreting PC2 as observation scores, the group-mean distribution has MN highest (+1.80), then OBA (+0.69) and SG (+0.62); YBA is lowest (-1.12), while EN and LN straddle/average below zero (-0.70, -0.44). These score signs are conventional, not intrinsic. A direct natural-language hypothesis is that symbolic-capital indicators co-vary positively with the dominant capital axis, but house size is more distinctive on the second axis, while dagger and hatchet/sword variation concentrates more on PC1. The direction “positive” is only after anchoring PC1 to positive hatchet/sword loading and PC2 to positive house-size loading.

**Shared evidence:**

Executed `analysis.py`. The 39 unique BCE dates run from 4300 to 500, every consecutive row differs by 100 years (38 intervals), and there are 6 period groups (EN 9 rows, MN 5, SG 7, LN 6, OBA 5, YBA 7). Data-feature variances are essentially one (mostly 0.97–1.00; ZSichel 0.289), consistent with source z values and making standardized-versus-covariance PCA a modest sensitivity. Standardized PCA explains 30.33% and 19.47% of variance on PC1 and PC2. Symbolic indicator PC1 eigenvector coefficients are 0.299 (hatchets/swords), 0.266 (daggers), 0.225 (house size); PC2 coefficients are 0.054, 0.074, 0.327. Feature-score correlations on PC1 are 0.570, 0.507, 0.429; on PC2, 0.083, 0.113, 0.500, respectively. Raw covariance PCA gives similar eigenvector coefficients: PC1 0.301, 0.279, 0.228; PC2 0.067, 0.120, 0.364. Standardized PC2 group means/ranges: MN +1.802 (1.315 to 2.503), OBA +0.686 (-0.724 to 2.136), SG +0.619 (0.198 to 1.108), LN -0.438 (-0.952 to 0.340), EN -0.697 (-3.648 to 2.010), YBA -1.124 (-1.671 to 0.265). Output script: `analysis.py`.

**Assumptions:** Pooled PCA across the 39 chronological bins is the most plausible reading because there is one row per 100-year slice; a PCA refit independently in each bin is mathematically impossible with one observation per bin.; The 12 capital-indicator columns are the intended PCA variables; BCE, group, and color are identifiers/annotations rather than features.; The source columns are already z-valued and their near-unit variances justify standardized PCA as a reasonable baseline; raw covariance PCA is provided as a check.; PC1 was oriented so ZAxtSchwert is positive and PC2 so Zhausgr is positive solely to make descriptions interpretable; all eigenvector and score signs can be jointly reversed without changing the PCA.

**Uncertainties:** No study analysis code/notebook is present to verify exact preprocessing or whether the original description used loading coefficients, feature-score correlations, or observation scores.; “Distribution across PC2” is ambiguous: feature loadings/correlations describe which variables define PC2, while group-level score summaries describe the temporal positions of bins. Both are included distinctly.; The per-group PCA sensitivity is not directly comparable to pooled PCA; groups contain few rows, and features constant within some groups, so orientations/loadings are unstable.; Group-level PC2 contrasts are descriptive and should not be interpreted as independent evidence or causal period effects.

### 3. Researcher — conclude

Hypothesis: In a pooled PCA of the 39 consecutive 100-year observations, house size, daggers, and hatchets/swords all align moderately with PC1. With PC1 oriented so the hatchet/sword loading is positive, their standardized loadings are 0.225, 0.266, and 0.299, respectively. House size is the symbolic indicator most associated with PC2 (loading 0.327; feature-score correlation 0.500), while dagger and hatchet/sword associations with PC2 are weak (loadings 0.074 and 0.054; correlations 0.113 and 0.083). Thus the weapons align principally with PC1, while house size contributes more to the second dimension. If “distribution across PC2” refers instead to temporal observation scores, group means are highest for MN (+1.80), followed by OBA (+0.69) and SG (+0.62), and lowest for YBA (−1.12); these directions depend on orienting PC2 to positive house-size loading and are descriptive, not intrinsic or causal. The analysis parsed comma-decimal values, verified the 100-year spacing, ran pooled PCA on the 12 capital indicators, and checked the target-variable results against raw-covariance PCA, which gave similar loadings. Exact original preprocessing and the intended meaning of “distribution” are undocumented, so the component interpretation is a transparent hypothesis rather than a confirmed study result.

**Decision:** stop

**Review phase:** result_review; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** The reported analysis addresses the requested symbolic variables and distinguishes feature loadings/correlations from observation-score distributions. It reports the 100-year structure, PCA workflow, sensitivity check, sign conventions, and relevant uncertainty; no unresolved mismatch with the objective is evident.

**Open objectives:** None

  "arguments": "{\"summary\": \"Run PCA using NumPy and report component loadings and group scores\", \"command\": \"cat > /tmp/pca.py <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\np=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str); features=p.columns[3:]\\nX=p[features].apply(lambda c: pd.to_numeric(c.str.replace(',','.',regex=False))).to_numpy(); targets=['ZAxtSchwert','ZDolch','Zhausgr']; ti=[list(features).index(s) for s in targets]\\ndef pca(A):\\n cov=np.cov(A,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); order=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[order][:2]; vec=vec[:,order][:,:2]\\n scores=A@vec\\n return vals,vec,scores\\nfor name,Z in [('covariance',X),('standardized',(X-X.mean(0))/X.std(0,ddof=1))]:\\n vals,vec,scores=pca(Z)\\n print('\\\\n',name,'variance ratio',np.round(vals/np.linalg.eigvalsh(np.cov(Z,rowvar=False)).sum(),4))\\n print('target eigenvector loadings',dict((targets[i],np.round(vec[ti[i],:],4).tolist()) for i in range(3)))\\n # PC1 positive anchor ZAxtSchwert; PC2 sign anchor dagger loading positive\\n if vec[ti[0],0]<0: vec[:,0]*=-1; scores[:,0]*=-1\\n if vec[ti[1],1]<0: vec[:,1]*=-1; scores[:,1]*=-1\\n print('oriented targets loadings',dict((targets[i],np.round(vec[ti[i],:],4).tolist()) for i in range(3)))\\n pp=p[['BCE','group']].copy();pp['PC1']=scores[:,0]; pp['PC2']=scores[:,1]\\n print('overall PC2 min/max',round(scores[:,1].min(),3),round(scores[:,1].max(),3))\\n print(pp.groupby('group').agg(n=('PC2','size'),BCE_min=('BCE','min'),BCE_max=('BCE','max'),PC1mean=('PC1','mean'),PC2mean=('PC2','mean'),PC2min=('PC2','min'),PC2max=('PC2','max')).round(3).to_string())\\n# slice-specific candidate PCA on within-group observations; dimensions too small? calculate loading stability\\nprint('\\\\nGroup-wise PCA standardized rows')\\nfor g,inds in p.groupby('group').groups.items():\\n xx=X[list(inds),:]; sd=xx.std(0,ddof=1)\\n # PCA fails constant features: standardize nonconstant variables only; feature-level scale appropriate but n 5-9\\n keep=sd>1e-10; z=(xx[:,keep]-xx[:,keep].mean(0))/sd[keep]\\n va,ve,sc=pca(z)\\n # orient PC1 by ZAxtSchwert if nonconstant; PC2 by dagger; report matching loadings\\n fkeep=np.array(features)[keep].tolist();\\n for j,anch in [(0,'ZAxtSchwert'),(1,'ZDolch')]:\\n  if anch in fkeep and ve[fkeep.index(anch),j]<0: ve[:,j]*=-1\\n d={f:np.round(ve[fkeep.index(f),:],3).tolist() if f in fkeep else None for f in targets}\\n print(g,'n',len(inds),'variable_count',sum(keep),'variance',np.round(va/np.linalg.eigvalsh(np.cov(z,rowvar=False)).sum(),3),'loadings',d)\\nPY\\npython /tmp/pca.py\", \"timeout_seconds\": 30}",

}

  "arguments": "{\"summary\": \"Produce reproducible PCA script and complete candidate diagnostics\", \"command\": \"cat > analysis.py <<'PY'\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"Reproducible descriptive PCA check of staged capital.csv.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nimport numpy as np\\nimport pandas as pd\\n\\nPATH = 'data/capital.csv'\\ndf = pd.read_csv(PATH, dtype=str)\\nfeatures = list(df.columns[3:])\\n# Source file uses decimal comma in several columns.\\nXdf = df[features].apply(lambda s: pd.to_numeric(s.str.replace(',', '.', regex=False)))\\nX = Xdf.to_numpy(float)\\ntargets = ['ZAxtSchwert', 'ZDolch', 'Zhausgr']\\nidx = [features.index(s) for s in targets]\\n\\ndef pca(a):\\n    ev, vectors = np.linalg.eigh(np.cov(a, rowvar=False))\\n    order = np.argsort(ev)[::-1]\\n    ev, vectors = ev[order], vectors[:, order]\\n    return ev[:2], vectors[:, :2]\\n\\nprint(f'rows={len(df)} unique_BCE={df.BCE.nunique()} BCE_step_counts={df.BCE.astype(int).diff().value_counts().to_dict()}')\\nprint('groups:', df.groupby('group').agg(n=('BCE','size'), oldest=('BCE','max'), newest=('BCE','min')).to_dict('index'))\\nprint('feature variances:', Xdf.var().round(3).to_dict())\\nfor method, A in [('covariance', X), ('correlation/standardized', (X-X.mean(0))/X.std(0,ddof=1))]:\\n    ev,V = pca(A)\\n    # PCA orientation is arbitrary: anchor PC1 on hatchet/sword, PC2 on house size.\\n    S=A@V\\n    if V[idx[0],0] < 0: V[:,0]*=-1; S[:,0]*=-1\\n    if V[idx[2],1] < 0: V[:,1]*=-1; S[:,1]*=-1\\n    print(f'\\\\n{method}: top-two variance fractions={np.round(ev/np.linalg.eigvalsh(np.cov(A,rowvar=False)).sum(),4)}')\\n    cor=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,0])[0:len(features),len(features)]\\n    cor2=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,1])[0:len(features),len(features)]\\n    for name,i in zip(targets,idx):\\n        print(f'{name}: PC1 eigenvector={V[i,0]:.4f}; PC1 score-correlation={cor[i]:.4f}; PC2 eigenvector={V[i,1]:.4f}; PC2 score-correlation={cor2[i]:.4f}')\\n    tab=df[['BCE','group']].copy(); tab['PC1score']=S[:,0]; tab['PC2score']=S[:,1]\\n    print('PC2 row-score distribution by group:')\\n    print(tab.groupby('group').agg(n=('PC2score','size'),PC2mean=('PC2score','mean'),PC2min=('PC2score','min'),PC2max=('PC2score','max')).round(3).to_string())\\n# Per-group refit is only a sensitivity check; not equivalent to one time slice per row.\\n# Groups have 5-9 rows and some symbolic features are constant, so rankings vary and\\n# cannot define a single chronological score axis.\\nPY\\npython analysis.py\", \"timeout_seconds\": 30}",

### S5. Assistant

Hypothesis: In a pooled PCA of the 39 consecutive 100-year observations, house size, daggers, and hatchets/swords all align moderately with PC1. With PC1 oriented so the hatchet/sword loading is positive, their standardized loadings are 0.225, 0.266, and 0.299, respectively. House size is the symbolic indicator most associated with PC2 (loading 0.327; feature-score correlation 0.500), while dagger and hatchet/sword associations with PC2 are weak (loadings 0.074 and 0.054; correlations 0.113 and 0.083). Thus the weapons align principally with PC1, while house size contributes more to the second dimension. If “distribution across PC2” refers instead to temporal observation scores, group means are highest for MN (+1.80), followed by OBA (+0.69) and SG (+0.62), and lowest for YBA (−1.12); these directions depend on orienting PC2 to positive house-size loading and are descriptive, not intrinsic or causal. The analysis parsed comma-decimal values, verified the 100-year spacing, ran pooled PCA on the 12 capital indicators, and checked the target-variable results against raw-covariance PCA, which gave similar loadings. Exact original preprocessing and the intended meaning of “distribution” are undocumented, so the component interpretation is a transparent hypothesis rather than a confirmed study result.

  "command": "cat > analysis.py <<'PY'\n\"\"\"Reproducible descriptive PCA check of staged capital.csv.\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nPATH = 'data/capital.csv'\ndf = pd.read_csv(PATH, dtype=str)\nfeatures = list(df.columns[3:])\n# Source file uses decimal comma in several columns.\nXdf = df[features].apply(lambda s: pd.to_numeric(s.str.replace(',', '.', regex=False)))\nX = Xdf.to_numpy(float)\ntargets = ['ZAxtSchwert', 'ZDolch', 'Zhausgr']\nidx = [features.index(s) for s in targets]\n\ndef pca(a):\n    ev, vectors = np.linalg.eigh(np.cov(a, rowvar=False))\n    order = np.argsort(ev)[::-1]\n    ev, vectors = ev[order], vectors[:, order]\n    return ev[:2], vectors[:, :2]\n\nprint(f'rows={len(df)} unique_BCE={df.BCE.nunique()} BCE_step_counts={df.BCE.astype(int).diff().value_counts().to_dict()}')\nprint('groups:', df.groupby('group').agg(n=('BCE','size'), oldest=('BCE','max'), newest=('BCE','min')).to_dict('index'))\nprint('feature variances:', Xdf.var().round(3).to_dict())\nfor method, A in [('covariance', X), ('correlation/standardized', (X-X.mean(0))/X.std(0,ddof=1))]:\n    ev,V = pca(A)\n    # PCA orientation is arbitrary: anchor PC1 on hatchet/sword, PC2 on house size.\n    S=A@V\n    if V[idx[0],0] < 0: V[:,0]*=-1; S[:,0]*=-1\n    if V[idx[2],1] < 0: V[:,1]*=-1; S[:,1]*=-1\n    print(f'\\n{method}: top-two variance fractions={np.round(ev/np.linalg.eigvalsh(np.cov(A,rowvar=False)).sum(),4)}')\n    cor=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,0])[0:len(features),len(features)]\n    cor2=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,1])[0:len(features),len(features)]\n    for name,i in zip(targets,idx):\n        print(f'{name}: PC1 eigenvector={V[i,0]:.4f}; PC1 score-correlation={cor[i]:.4f}; PC2 eigenvector={V[i,1]:.4f}; PC2 score-correlation={cor2[i]:.4f}')\n    tab=df[['BCE','group']].copy(); tab['PC1score']=S[:,0]; tab['PC2score']=S[:,1]\n    print('PC2 row-score distribution by group:')\n    print(tab.groupby('group').agg(n=('PC2score','size'),PC2mean=('PC2score','mean'),PC2min=('PC2score','min'),PC2max=('PC2score','max')).round(3).to_string())\n# Per-group refit is only a sensitivity check; not equivalent to one time slice per row.\n# Groups have 5-9 rows and some symbolic features are constant, so rankings vary and\n# cannot define a single chronological score axis.\nPY\npython analysis.py",
  "timeout_seconds": 30
}

Human impact or landscape openness, respectively, as reflected in the pollen data, can be used as a demographic indicator based on the assumption that an increasing population density leads to increasing woodland clearance due to an increasing demand for resources including wood, agricultural land and settlement areas. The 'Belau_PC1' of pollen data (pollen_openness_score_Belau_Woserin_Feeser_et_al_2019.csv) has been assumed to reflect openness. Original openness score through 'Belau_PC1', 100-year smoothed openness score, and linear interpolation of openness score have been used to signify growth. The original openness score (Belau_PC1), the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_inter_100), and the linear interpolation of the openness score (pollen_inter) have been used to signify openness. 6. Demographic growth manifests itself in growth set bringing with it a further opening of the landscape. Opening of landscape corresponds to higher growth rates. During the Early Neolithic, we are dealing with a population growth that goes hand in hand with the opening up of vegetation and the cultivation of the landscape. The growth rate is defined as the percentage change of the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_grate_100). 7. nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.

invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.

nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

S[:,0]*=-1\\n    if V[idx[2],1] < 0: V[:,1]*=-1; S[:,1]*=-1\\n    print(f'\\\\n{method}: top-two variance fractions={np.round(ev/np.linalg.eigvalsh(np.cov(A,rowvar=False)).sum(),4)}')\\n    cor=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,0])[0:len(features),len(features)]\\n    cor2=np.corrcoef(A.T,S[:,1])[0:len(features),len(features)]\\n    for name,i in zip(targets,idx):\\n        print(f'{name}: PC1 eigenvector={V[i,0]:.4f}; PC1 score-correlation={cor[i]:.4f}; PC2 eigenvector={V[i,1]:.4f}; PC2 score-correlation={cor2[i]:.4f}')\\n    tab=df[['BCE','group']].copy(); tab['PC1score']=S[:,0]; tab['PC2score']=S[:,1]\\n    print('PC2 row-score distribution by group:')\\n    print(tab.groupby('group').agg(n=('PC2score','size'),PC2mean=('PC2score','mean'),PC2min=('PC2score','min'),PC2max=('PC2score','max')).round(3).to_string())\\n# Per-group refit is only a sensitivity check; PC1 score-correlation={cor[i]:.4f}; PC2 eigenvector={V[i,1]:.4f}; PC2 score-correlation={cor2[i]:.4f}')\\n    tab=df[['BCE','group']].copy(); tab['PC1score']=S[:,0]; tab['PC2score']=S[:,1]\\n    print('PC2 row-score distribution by group:')\\n    print(tab.groupby('group').agg(n=('PC2score','size'),PC2mean=('PC2score','mean'),PC2min=('PC2score','min'),PC2max=('PC2score','max')).round(3).to_string())\\n# Per-group refit is only a sensitivity check; This Principal component analysis (PCA) is on the forms of capital. The values of the individual elements of the forms of capital form the attributes.,
        "WorkflowA": None,
        "HypoB": Across the pooled 100-year observations, house size, daggers, and hatchets/swords all have moderate positive PC1 loadings when the axis is oriented to make the hatchet/sword loading positive (standardized PCA: 0.225, 0.266, 0.299). Their PC1 score correlations are approximately 0.43, 0.51, and 0.57. House size is much more associated with PC2 (loading 0.327; score correlation 0.500) than daggers (0.074; 0.113) or hatchets/swords (0.054; 0.083), suggesting it is the principal symbolic indicator distinguishing the second dimension. PC2 observation scores vary across time/group slices: MN averages highest, followed by OBA and SG, with YBA lowest; this direction depends on the stated sign convention. The result supports a hypothesis of shared symbolic-capital alignment on PC1 and a stronger house-size contribution to PC2, not a claim that component signs are absolute.,
        "WorkflowB": Read capital.csv with values as strings, converted comma decimal separators to numeric, verified 39 rows at consecutive 100-year BCE intervals and six period-group labels, then computed pooled PCA via eigendecomposition of the feature covariance matrix after z-standardizing all 12 capital indicators. Reported eigenvector coefficients and feature-score correlations separately. Also ran raw covariance PCA as a sensitivity analysis; results for the three target variables were similar. Compared group-level PC2 score ranges. The per-group PCA alternative is unstable/not a defensible interpretation of one score per time slice because each row is already a 100-year bin and each group contains only 5–9 rows.
